<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove Brennan's conjecture: if a simply connected plane domain admits a conformal bijection <i>φ</i> onto the unit disk, then $`|\varphi'|^s`$ is area-integrable for every $`4/3\lt s\lt 4`$. We also prove the sharp inverse-square integral-means exponent $`B_{\mathcal S}(-2)=1`$ for the normalized schlicht class $`\mathcal S`$.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that $$\label{eq:main-uniform} M_{-2}[f'](r)\le C_\varepsilon(1-r)^{-1-\varepsilon} \qquad\left(f\in\mathcal S,\ \frac12\le r<1\right).$$ Moreover, $B_{\mathcal S}(-2)=1$.* *Let $W\subset\mathbb C$ be any simply connected domain whose boundary in the Riemann sphere contains at least two points. Every conformal bijection $\varphi:W\to\mathbb D$ satisfies $$\int_W\|\varphi'(z)\|^s\,dA(z)<\infty \qquad\left(\frac43<s<4\right).$$ Equivalently, every univalent map $f:\mathbb D\to\mathbb C$ satisfies $$\int_{\mathbb D}\|f'(w)\|^t\,dA(w)<\infty \qquad\left(-2<t<\frac23\right).$$
  - Explanation: The uniform inverse-square radial estimate yields the sharp spectral endpoint and the full open area-integrability range for conformal bijections and their inverses.
- **C2** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-cor-sharp-endpoints)): The Koebe function $f(z)=z/(1-z)^2$ belongs to $\mathcal S$ and satisfies $$\int_{\mathbb D}\|f'\|^{-2}\,dA=\infty, \qquad \int_{\mathbb D}\|f'\|^{2/3}\,dA=\infty.$$ Its inverse therefore fails area integrability at both $s=4$ and $s=4/3$.
  - Explanation: The Koebe map makes both boundary exponents fail, so the area-integrability interval must remain open.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-classical-univalence)): If $f(z)=z+a_2z^2+\cdots$ belongs to $\mathcal S$, then $\|a_2\|\le2$ and, for $\|z\|=r<1$, $$\frac{1-r}{(1+r)^3}\le \|f'(z)\|\le\frac{1+r}{(1-r)^3}, \qquad \frac r4\le \|f(z)\|\le\frac{r}{(1-r)^2}. \label{prelim:disk-bounds}$$ Every univalent holomorphic map $g$ on an open disk $B(z_0,R)$ satisfies $$B\bigl(g(z_0),R\|g'(z_0)\|/4\bigr)\subset g(B(z_0,R)). \label{prelim:quarter}$$
  - Explanation: Classical coefficient, derivative, growth, and disk-inclusion estimates hold without boundary regularity assumptions.
- **C4** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-compact-class)): The space $\mathcal K$ is compact and metrizable. For every integer $j\ge0$, the evaluations $(F,z)\mapsto F^{(j)}(z)$ and $(F,z)\mapsto Q_F^{(j)}(z)$ are jointly continuous on $\mathcal K\times\mathbb H$ and bounded on $\mathcal K\times E$ for every compact $E\subset\mathbb H$. For every $C_0\ge0$, $$\|Q_F(x+iy)\|\le\frac{(C_0^2+4)^2}{y} \quad\bigl(F\in\mathcal K,\ \|x\|\le C_0,\ 0<y\le1\bigr). \label{prelim:strip-bound}$$ In particular the bound is $64/y$ when $\|x\|\le2$. Also, $$\|F(z)\|\ge\frac1{16} \quad\text{if }F\in\mathcal K\text{ and }\|m(z)\|\ge\frac12. \label{prelim:zero-separation}$$
  - Explanation: Normalized half-plane maps form a compact family with continuous derivative evaluations and explicit local bounds.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-transfer-growth)): For $z,w\in\mathbb H$ and $F\in\mathcal K$, $$Q_{T_zF}(w)=\frac{Q_F(z\cdot w)}{Q_F(z)},\qquad T_w(T_zF)=T_{z\cdot w}F,\qquad A_zA_w=A_{z\cdot w}. \label{prelim:composition}$$ Each $A_z$ is bounded on $C(\mathcal K)$, and for fixed $\Phi\in C(\mathcal K)$ the map $z\mapsto A_z\Phi$ is continuous in the supremum norm. For $n\ge0$, put $v_n=2^{-n}$ and $u_{n,j}=-1+(2j+1)v_n$, $0\le j<2^n$. Then $$L^n=v_n\sum_{j=0}^{2^n-1} A_{u_{n,j}+iv_n},\qquad \\|L^n\\|=\max_{F\in\mathcal K}L^n\mathbf1(F). \label{prelim:dyadic-grid}$$ The limit $$\rho=\lim_{n\to\infty}\\|L^n\\|^{1/n} =\inf_{n\ge1}\\|L^n\\|^{1/n} \label{prelim:rho}$$ exists and satisfies $1\le\rho\le4$. If $\lambda>\rho$, there is $C_\lambda<\infty$ such that $\\|L^n\\|\le C_\lambda\lambda^n$ for all $n\ge0$.
  - Explanation: Affine rescaling composes exactly, giving the dyadic sampling formula and the norm growth rate.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-disk-transfer)): If $\rho\le2$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that $$M_{-2}[f'](r)\le C_\varepsilon(1-r)^{-1-\varepsilon} \quad\bigl(f\in\mathcal S,\ 1/2\le r<1\bigr). \label{prelim:uniform-disk}$$ The constant depends only on $\varepsilon$.
  - Explanation: A bound on transfer growth controls disk inverse-square means uniformly over the normalized class.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-weighted-law)): Suppose that $\rho>2$, and put $\beta=\log_2\rho>1$. There is a Borel probability measure $P$ on $\mathcal K$ such that, for every $z=x+iy\in\mathbb H$ and every nonnegative Borel function $\Phi:\mathcal K\to[0,\infty]$, $$\mathbb E\!\left[\|Q_F(z)\|^2\Phi(T_zF)\right] =y^{-\beta}\mathbb E\Phi(F), \label{eq:weighted-covariance}$$ where $\mathbb E$ denotes expectation with respect to $P$.
  - Explanation: Hypothetical excess transfer growth produces a probability law with weighted affine covariance.
- **C8** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-law-moments)): Assume $\rho>2$, and let $P$ and $\beta$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:weighted-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weighted-law">7</a>. For $z=x+iy\in\mathbb H$, define $$q_F(z)=iy\frac{Q'_F(z)}{Q_F(z)} =-iy\frac{F''(z)}{F'(z)} =a_F(z)+ib_F(z), \label{eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable}$$ where $a_F,b_F$ are real. The variables $q_F(i)$ are bounded on $\mathcal K$, and $$\mathbb Ea_F(i)=-\frac\beta2, \qquad \mathbb Eb_F(i)=0, \qquad \mathbb E\|q_F(i)\|^2=\frac{\beta(\beta+1)}4. \label{eq:law-moments}$$ They also satisfy $$q_{T_zF}(w)=q_F(z\cdot w),\qquad z,w\in\mathbb H. \label{eq:q-affine-covariance}$$
  - Explanation: Differentiating the covariance identity determines the logarithmic-derivative moments under the hypothetical law.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-proper-superlevels)): For $P$-almost every $F\in\mathcal K$, for planar area-almost every $\xi\in\mathbb C$, the set $$\{z\in\mathbb H:V_\xi^F(z)\ge\delta\}$$ is compact in $\mathbb H$ for every $\delta>0$.
  - Explanation: Positive superlevels of the target potential are compact for almost every sampled map and almost every target.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-weighted-inverse-area)): The function $I:\mathcal K\to[0,\infty]$ is Borel and satisfies $\mathbb EI(F)\le256\pi$. In particular, $I(F)<\infty$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: The expected weighted inverse area is finite, implying almost sure finiteness.
- **C11** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-escape-trace)): Let $F\in\mathcal K$ satisfy $I(F)<\infty$. Then $$\frac{y^k}{\|F(x+iy)\|}\longrightarrow0 \qquad\text{as }\|x\|+y\longrightarrow\infty. \label{escape:tail}$$ For every compact $K\subset\mathbb C$ and $\epsilon>0$, $F^{-1}(K)\cap\{\Im z\ge\epsilon\}$ is compact in $\mathbb H$, and $$H_{F,K}:=\sup\{\Im z:F(z)\in K\}<\infty,$$ where the supremum of an empty set is assigned zero. Moreover, there is $C_{F,K}<\infty$ such that $$\int_{\mathbb R}\mathbf1_K(F(x+i\epsilon))\,dx \le C_{F,K}\qquad(\epsilon>0). \label{escape:trace}$$
  - Explanation: Finite weighted inverse area controls escape and the horizontal length of preimages of compact target sets.
- **C12** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-superlevel-borel)): The set of pairs $(F,\xi)\in\mathcal K\times\mathbb C$ for which every closed positive superlevel of $V_\xi^F$ is compact in $\mathbb H$ is Borel.
  - Explanation: Compactness of all positive superlevels is a Borel property of the map and target.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-jacobian-moments)): For every $F\in\mathcal K$ and $z\in\mathbb H$, $$k^2J_F(z)=k(k-1)+(2k-1)a_F(z)+\|q_F(z)\|^2. \label{eq:jacobian-formula}$$ The function $F\mapsto J_F(i)$ is bounded and continuous on $\mathcal K$, and $$k^2\mathbb EJ_F(i)=k(k-1)>0. \label{eq:positive-jacobian-mean}$$
  - Explanation: The normalized critical-map Jacobian has strictly positive expectation under the hypothetical weighted law.
- **C14** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-critical-signs)): Fix $F\in\mathcal K$ and $\xi\in\mathbb C$. Away from the unique possible pole of $V^F_\xi$, put $\ell=\log V^F_\xi$. Then $$\Delta\ell=-\frac{k}{y^2}, \label{eq:negative-laplacian}$$ and its critical points are exactly the solutions of $G_F(z)=\xi$. At such a point, $$\begin{aligned} V^F_\xi(z)&=k y^{k-1}\|Q_F(z)\|, \label{eq:critical-height}\\ \operatorname{Hess}\ell(z) &=\frac{k}{y^2} \begin{pmatrix} -(k+a_F(z))&b_F(z)\\ b_F(z)&k-1+a_F(z) \end{pmatrix}, \label{eq:critical-hessian}\\ \det\operatorname{Hess}\ell(z)&=-\frac{k^4}{y^4}J_F(z). \label{eq:critical-hessian-det} \end{aligned}$$ Consequently a solution with $J_F(z)>0$ is a nondegenerate saddle, and one with $J_F(z)<0$ is a nondegenerate maximum.
  - Explanation: The Jacobian sign separates nondegenerate saddles from maxima of the target potential.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-good-pairs)): The set $\mathcal G$ is Borel. For $P$-almost every $F$, its section $\{\xi:(F,\xi)\in\mathcal G\}$ has full planar area measure.* *For every $F\in\mathcal K$, $\xi\in\mathbb C$, $t=x_0+iy_0\in\mathbb H$, and $w\in\mathbb H$, set $\widetilde\xi=(\xi-F(t))/(y_0F'(t))$. Then $$\begin{aligned} G_{T_tF}(w)&=\frac{G_F(t\cdot w)-F(t)}{y_0F'(t)}, \label{eq:critical-map-covariance}\\ q_{T_tF}(w)&=q_F(t\cdot w),\qquad J_{T_tF}(w)=J_F(t\cdot w), \label{eq:jacobian-covariance}\\ V^{T_tF}_{\widetilde\xi}(w) &=y_0^{1-k}\|F'(t)\|V^F_\xi(t\cdot w). \label{eq:critical-height-covariance} \end{aligned}$$ In particular, $(F,\xi)\in\mathcal G$ if and only if $(T_tF,\widetilde\xi)\in\mathcal G$.
  - Explanation: Good pairs form a Borel set, have full-area target sections almost surely, and are preserved by affine source changes.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-critical-count)): Fix a good pair $(F,\xi)$. There are only finitely many critical points of $V^F_\xi$ with value at least $h$, for every $h>0$. For every such $h$, $$\#\{\text{maxima with }V^F_\xi\ge h\} \ \ge\ \#\{\text{saddles with }V^F_\xi\ge h\}. \label{eq:cumulative-critical-count}$$ The possible pole is excluded from the maximum count.
  - Explanation: For a good target, finite maxima outnumber saddles above each positive level, with the pole excluded from the maximum count.
- **C17** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-critical-pairing)): Let $$\mathcal D=\{(F,z)\in\mathcal K\times\mathbb H: J_F(z)>0,\ (F,G_F(z))\in\mathcal G\}.$$ There is a Borel map $R:\mathcal D\to\mathbb H$, written $R_F(z)=R(F,z)$, such that, for every $(F,z)\in\mathcal D$, with $z'=R_F(z)$ and $\xi=G_F(z)$, $$G_F(z')=\xi,\qquad J_F(z')<0,\qquad V^F_\xi(z')\ge V^F_\xi(z). \label{eq:pairing-guarantees}$$ For each fixed $F$, the map $R_F$ is injective on its domain. It is affine equivariant: if $t\in\mathbb H$ and $(F,t\cdot w)\in\mathcal D$, then $$(T_tF,w)\in\mathcal D,\qquad R_{T_tF}(w)=t^{\#}\cdot R_F(t\cdot w). \label{eq:pairing-covariance}$$
  - Explanation: Equal ranks assign each saddle a distinct maximum of at least the same height; this assignment is Borel and affine equivariant.
- **C18** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-root-exception)): Suppose $\rho>2$, let $P$ be the weighted law of Lemma <a href="#lem:weighted-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weighted-law">7</a>, and set $k=(\log_2\rho+3)/4$. Then $$\label{eq:root-exception} \mathbb E\!\left[ J_{F,+}(i)\, \mathbf 1_{\{(F,-i/k)\notin\mathcal G\}} \right]=0.$$
  - Explanation: Exceptional targets contribute no positive Jacobian weight at the normalized root.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-transfer-bound)): The dyadic transfer operator satisfies $\rho\leq2$.
  - Explanation: The transfer growth bound follows by contradicting the positive Jacobian expectation with the integrated saddle-to-maximum comparison.
- **C20** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-negative-linear-tail)): For every real $t\leq-2$ and every $\epsilon>0$, there is $C_{t,\epsilon}<\infty$ such that $$\label{eq:negative-linear-tail} M_t[f'](r)\leq C_{t,\epsilon}(1-r)^{-(\|t\|-1)-\epsilon} \qquad\left(f\in\mathcal S,\ \frac12\leq r<1\right).$$ Consequently, $$B_{\mathcal S}(t)=\|t\|-1\qquad(t\leq-2).$$
  - Explanation: The sharp inverse-square estimate and distortion give the full negative linear spectral tail.
- **C21** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-function-powers)): For every $0<p<1/2$ there is $C_p<\infty$ such that $$\label{eq:function-means} \frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi} \|f(re^{i\theta})\|^p\,d\theta\leq C_p \qquad(f\in\mathcal S,\ 0<r<1).$$ Moreover, for every $0\leq a<1$ and every $f\in\mathcal S$, $$\label{eq:function-area} \int_{\mathbb D}(1+\|f(z)\|)^a\,dA(z)<\infty.$$
  - Explanation: Small positive powers have uniformly bounded circular means and the stated function area powers are integrable.
- **C22** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-positive-derivative-powers)): For every $f\in\mathcal S$ and $0<t<2/3$, $$\int_{\mathbb D}\|f'(z)\|^t\,dA(z)<\infty.$$
  - Explanation: A weighted conformal area estimate and Hölder inequality give positive derivative-power integrability.
- **C23** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-sobolev-pullback)): Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb C$ be a simply connected domain whose boundary in the Riemann sphere has at least two points, and let $\varphi:\Omega\to\mathbb D$ be a conformal bijection. For $2<p<\infty$ and $4/3<s<4$, put $q=ps/(p+s-2)$. Then pullback $\varphi^*u=u\circ\varphi$ is bounded from $L_p^1(\mathbb D)$ to $L_q^1(\Omega)$. It is also bounded from $L_\infty^1(\mathbb D)$ to $L_s^1(\Omega)$ for every $4/3<s<4$. The finite bounds on gradient seminorms may depend on the map and the exponents.
  - Explanation: Derivative integrability yields bounded conformal pullback between the stated homogeneous Sobolev spaces; bounds may depend on the map and exponents.

          "exact_quote": "If $\\rho\\le2$, then for every $\\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\\varepsilon<\\infty$ such that $$M_{-2}[f'](r)\\le C_\\varepsilon(1-r)^{-1-\\varepsilon}\n \\quad\\bigl(f\\in\\mathcal S,\\ 1/2\\le r<1\\bigr).\n \\label{prelim:uniform-disk}$$ The constant depends only on $\\varepsilon$.",
          "source_id": "PM-072.1"
        }
      ],

      "kind": "conditional_result",
      "quantifiers": [],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-prop-disk-transfer"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-weighted-law",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose that $\\rho>2$, and put $\\beta=\\log_2\\rho>1$. There is a Borel probability measure $P$ on $\\mathcal K$ such that, for every $z=x+iy\\in\\mathbb H$ and every nonnegative Borel function $\\Phi:\\mathcal K\\to[0,\\infty]$, $$\\mathbb E\\!\\left[|Q_F(z)|^2\\Phi(T_zF)\\right]\n   =y^{-\\beta}\\mathbb E\\Phi(F),\n \\label{eq:weighted-covariance}$$ where $\\mathbb E$ denotes expectation with respect to $P$.",
        "source_id": "PM-072.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

          "exact_quote": "Suppose that $\\rho>2$, and put $\\beta=\\log_2\\rho>1$.           "source_id": "PM-072.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Suppose that $\\rho>2$, and put $\\beta=\\log_2\\rho>1$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-law-moments",
        "exact_quote": "Assume $\\rho>2$, and let $P$ and $\\beta$ be as in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:weighted-law\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:weighted-law\">7</a>. For $z=x+iy\\in\\mathbb H$, define $$q_F(z)=iy\\frac{Q'_F(z)}{Q_F(z)}\n       =-iy\\frac{F''(z)}{F'(z)}\n       =a_F(z)+ib_F(z),\n \\label{eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable}$$ where $a_F,b_F$ are real. The variables $q_F(i)$ are bounded on $\\mathcal K$, and $$\\mathbb Ea_F(i)=-\\frac\\beta2,\n \\qquad \\mathbb Eb_F(i)=0,\n \\qquad \\mathbb E|q_F(i)|^2=\\frac{\\beta(\\beta+1)}4.\n \\label{eq:law-moments}$$ They also satisfy $$q_{T_zF}(w)=q_F(z\\cdot w),\\qquad z,w\\in\\mathbb H.\n \\label{eq:q-affine-covariance}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-072.1"
      },

The variables $q_F(i)$ are bounded on $\\mathcal K$, and $$\\mathbb Ea_F(i)=-\\frac\\beta2,\n \\qquad \\mathbb Eb_F(i)=0,\n \\qquad \\mathbb E|q_F(i)|^2=\\frac{\\beta(\\beta+1)}4.\n \\label{eq:law-moments}$$ They also satisfy $$q_{T_zF}(w)=q_F(z\\cdot w),\\qquad z,w\\in\\mathbb H.\n \\label{eq:q-affine-covariance}$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-escape-trace",
        "exact_quote": "Let $F\\in\\mathcal K$ satisfy $I(F)<\\infty$. Then $$\\frac{y^k}{|F(x+iy)|}\\longrightarrow0\n       \\qquad\\text{as }|x|+y\\longrightarrow\\infty.\n \\label{escape:tail}$$ For every compact $K\\subset\\mathbb C$ and $\\epsilon>0$, $F^{-1}(K)\\cap\\{\\Im z\\ge\\epsilon\\}$ is compact in $\\mathbb H$, and $$H_{F,K}:=\\sup\\{\\Im z:F(z)\\in K\\}<\\infty,$$ where the supremum of an empty set is assigned zero. Moreover, there is $C_{F,K}<\\infty$ such that $$\\int_{\\mathbb R}\\mathbf1_K(F(x+i\\epsilon))\\,dx\n       \\le C_{F,K}\\qquad(\\epsilon>0).\n \\label{escape:trace}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-072.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-jacobian-moments",
        "exact_quote": "For every $F\\in\\mathcal K$ and $z\\in\\mathbb H$, $$k^2J_F(z)=k(k-1)+(2k-1)a_F(z)+|q_F(z)|^2.\n \\label{eq:jacobian-formula}$$ The function $F\\mapsto J_F(i)$ is bounded and continuous on $\\mathcal K$, and $$k^2\\mathbb EJ_F(i)=k(k-1)>0.\n \\label{eq:positive-jacobian-mean}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-072.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

          "source_id": "PM-072.1"

      "exact_statement": "For every $F\\in\\mathcal K$ and $z\\in\\mathbb H$, $$k^2J_F(z)=k(k-1)+(2k-1)a_F(z)+|q_F(z)|^2.\n \\label{eq:jacobian-formula}$$ The function $F\\mapsto J_F(i)$ is bounded and continuous on $\\mathcal K$, and $$k^2\\mathbb EJ_F(i)=k(k-1)>0.\n \\label{eq:positive-jacobian-mean}$$",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-critical-signs",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $F\\in\\mathcal K$ and $\\xi\\in\\mathbb C$. Away from the unique possible pole of $V^F_\\xi$, put $\\ell=\\log V^F_\\xi$. Then $$\\Delta\\ell=-\\frac{k}{y^2},\n \\label{eq:negative-laplacian}$$ and its critical points are exactly the solutions of $G_F(z)=\\xi$. At such a point, $$\\begin{aligned}\n V^F_\\xi(z)&=k y^{k-1}|Q_F(z)|,\n \\label{eq:critical-height}\\\\\n \\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)\n &=\\frac{k}{y^2}\n \\begin{pmatrix}\n  -(k+a_F(z))&b_F(z)\\\\\n  b_F(z)&k-1+a_F(z)\n \\end{pmatrix},\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian}\\\\\n \\det\\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)&=-\\frac{k^4}{y^4}J_F(z).\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian-det}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently a solution with $J_F(z)>0$ is a nondegenerate saddle, and one with $J_F(z)<0$ is a nondegenerate maximum.",
        "source_id": "PM-072.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-critical-signs",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $F\\in\\mathcal K$ and $\\xi\\in\\mathbb C$. Away from the unique possible pole of $V^F_\\xi$, put $\\ell=\\log V^F_\\xi$. Then $$\\Delta\\ell=-\\frac{k}{y^2},\n \\label{eq:negative-laplacian}$$ and its critical points are exactly the solutions of $G_F(z)=\\xi$. At such a point, $$\\begin{aligned}\n V^F_\\xi(z)&=k y^{k-1}|Q_F(z)|,\n \\label{eq:critical-height}\\\\\n \\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)\n &=\\frac{k}{y^2}\n \\begin{pmatrix}\n  -(k+a_F(z))&b_F(z)\\\\\n  b_F(z)&k-1+a_F(z)\n \\end{pmatrix},\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian}\\\\\n \\det\\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)&=-\\frac{k^4}{y^4}J_F(z).\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian-det}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently a solution with $J_F(z)>0$ is a nondegenerate saddle, and one with $J_F(z)<0$ is a nondegenerate maximum.",
          "source_id": "PM-072.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Fix $F\\in\\mathcal K$ and $\\xi\\in\\mathbb C$. Away from the unique possible pole of $V^F_\\xi$, put $\\ell=\\log V^F_\\xi$. Then $$\\Delta\\ell=-\\frac{k}{y^2},\n \\label{eq:negative-laplacian}$$ and its critical points are exactly the solutions of $G_F(z)=\\xi$. At such a point, $$\\begin{aligned}\n V^F_\\xi(z)&=k y^{k-1}|Q_F(z)|,\n \\label{eq:critical-height}\\\\\n \\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)\n &=\\frac{k}{y^2}\n \\begin{pmatrix}\n  -(k+a_F(z))&b_F(z)\\\\\n  b_F(z)&k-1+a_F(z)\n \\end{pmatrix},\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian}\\\\\n \\det\\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)&=-\\frac{k^4}{y^4}J_F(z).\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian-det}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently a solution with $J_F(z)>0$ is a nondegenerate saddle, and one with $J_F(z)<0$ is a nondegenerate maximum.",
      "explanation": "The Jacobian sign separates nondegenerate saddles from maxima of the target potential.",

      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-critical-signs",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $F\\in\\mathcal K$ and $\\xi\\in\\mathbb C$. Away from the unique possible pole of $V^F_\\xi$, put $\\ell=\\log V^F_\\xi$. Then $$\\Delta\\ell=-\\frac{k}{y^2},\n \\label{eq:negative-laplacian}$$ and its critical points are exactly the solutions of $G_F(z)=\\xi$. At such a point, $$\\begin{aligned}\n V^F_\\xi(z)&=k y^{k-1}|Q_F(z)|,\n \\label{eq:critical-height}\\\\\n \\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)\n &=\\frac{k}{y^2}\n \\begin{pmatrix}\n  -(k+a_F(z))&b_F(z)\\\\\n  b_F(z)&k-1+a_F(z)\n \\end{pmatrix},\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian}\\\\\n \\det\\operatorname{Hess}\\ell(z)&=-\\frac{k^4}{y^4}J_F(z).\n \\label{eq:critical-hessian-det}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Consequently a solution with $J_F(z)>0$ is a nondegenerate saddle, and one with $J_F(z)<0$ is a nondegenerate maximum.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-good-pairs",
        "exact_quote": "The set $\\mathcal G$ is Borel. For $P$-almost every $F$, its section $\\{\\xi:(F,\\xi)\\in\\mathcal G\\}$ has full planar area measure.*\n\n\n\n\n*For every $F\\in\\mathcal K$, $\\xi\\in\\mathbb C$, $t=x_0+iy_0\\in\\mathbb H$, and $w\\in\\mathbb H$, set $\\widetilde\\xi=(\\xi-F(t))/(y_0F'(t))$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n G_{T_tF}(w)&=\\frac{G_F(t\\cdot w)-F(t)}{y_0F'(t)},\n \\label{eq:critical-map-covariance}\\\\\n q_{T_tF}(w)&=q_F(t\\cdot w),\\qquad J_{T_tF}(w)=J_F(t\\cdot w),\n \\label{eq:jacobian-covariance}\\\\\n V^{T_tF}_{\\widetilde\\xi}(w)\n &=y_0^{1-k}|F'(t)|V^F_\\xi(t\\cdot w).\n \\label{eq:critical-height-covariance}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $(F,\\xi)\\in\\mathcal G$ if and only if $(T_tF,\\widetilde\\xi)\\in\\mathcal G$.",
        "source_id": "PM-072.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-good-pairs",
          "exact_quote": "The set $\\mathcal G$ is Borel. For $P$-almost every $F$, its section $\\{\\xi:(F,\\xi)\\in\\mathcal G\\}$ has full planar area measure.*\n\n\n\n\n*For every $F\\in\\mathcal K$, $\\xi\\in\\mathbb C$, $t=x_0+iy_0\\in\\mathbb H$, and $w\\in\\mathbb H$, set $\\widetilde\\xi=(\\xi-F(t))/(y_0F'(t))$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n G_{T_tF}(w)&=\\frac{G_F(t\\cdot w)-F(t)}{y_0F'(t)},\n \\label{eq:critical-map-covariance}\\\\\n q_{T_tF}(w)&=q_F(t\\cdot w),\\qquad J_{T_tF}(w)=J_F(t\\cdot w),\n \\label{eq:jacobian-covariance}\\\\\n V^{T_tF}_{\\widetilde\\xi}(w)\n &=y_0^{1-k}|F'(t)|V^F_\\xi(t\\cdot w).\n \\label{eq:critical-height-covariance}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $(F,\\xi)\\in\\mathcal G$ if and only if $(T_tF,\\widetilde\\xi)\\in\\mathcal G$.",
          "source_id": "PM-072.1"

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Unit disk and planar area measure** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Let $\mathbb D=\{z\in\mathbb C:\|z\|<1\}$, and write $dA=dx\,dy$ for planar Lebesgue measure.
- **Domain and univalent map** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A domain is a connected open subset of $\mathbb C$; a univalent map is an injective holomorphic map.
- **Normalized schlicht class** ([source](#sec:introduction)): To state its radial form, let $$\mathcal S=\{f:\mathbb D\to\mathbb C\text{ univalent}:f(0)=0,\ f'(0)=1\}$$ be the normalized schlicht class.
- **Derivative integral means and spectrum** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For $f\in\mathcal S$, $t\in\mathbb R$, and $0<r<1$, define $$M_t[f'](r)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi} \|f'(re^{i\theta})\|^t\,d\theta,$$ and put $$\begin{aligned} \beta_f(t)&=\limsup_{r\uparrow1} \frac{\log M_t[f'](r)}{\log(1/(1-r))},\\ B_{\mathcal S}(t)&=\sup_{f\in\mathcal S}\beta_f(t). \end{aligned}$$
- **Half-plane normalized class** ([source](#prelim:normalization)): Write $$\mathbb H=\{x+iy:x\in\mathbb R,\ y>0\},\qquad m(z)=\frac{z-i}{z+i},$$ and let $$\mathcal K=\{F:\mathbb H\to\mathbb C\text{ univalent}:F(i)=0,\ F'(i)=1\}.$$
- **Inverse derivative** ([source](#prelim:normalization)): For $F\in\mathcal K$, define the holomorphic function $$Q_F(z)=\frac1{F'(z)}.$$
- **Affine rerooting** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For $F\in\mathcal K$ define $$T_zF(w)=\frac{F(z\cdot w)-F(z)}{yF'(z)}. \label{prelim:rerooting}$$
- **Weighted transfer operator** ([source](#pm-h-8)): On the space $C(\mathcal K)$ of continuous complex-valued functions, equipped with its supremum norm, define $$A_z\Phi(F)=\|Q_F(z)\|^2\Phi(T_zF),\qquad L=\frac12\bigl(A_{-1/2+i/2}+A_{1/2+i/2}\bigr). \label{prelim:operators}$$
- **Target potential** ([source](#sec:escape)): Set $$k=\frac{\beta+3}{4}>1,\qquad V_\xi^F(x+iy)=\frac{y^k}{\|F(x+iy)-\xi\|} \quad(F\in\mathcal K,\ \xi\in\mathbb C), \label{escape:potential}$$ with value $+\infty$ at the unique possible point where $F(z)=\xi$.
- **Critical map** ([source](#pm-h-20)): Define the smooth real map $G_F:\mathbb H\to\mathbb C$ by $$G_F(z)=F(z)-\frac{iy}{k}F'(z),\qquad z=x+iy. \label{eq:critical-map}$$
- **Normalized Jacobian** ([source](#pm-h-20)): We define its normalized signed Jacobian by $$J_F(z)=\frac{\det DG_F(z)}{\|F'(z)\|^2}. \label{eq:normalized-jacobian}$$
- **Good pair** ([source](#stmt-def-good-pair)): A pair $(F,\xi)\in\mathcal K\times\mathbb C$ is *good* if every closed positive superlevel of $V^F_\xi$ is compact in $\mathbb H$, and $\xi$ is a regular value of $G_F$. Thus every solution $G_F(z)=\xi$ must satisfy $J_F(z)\ne0$; a target with no such solution is regular. Write $\mathcal G$ for the set of good pairs.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-compact-class) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-compact-class): Normal-family compactness and classical distortion -> Compact normalized class
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-transfer-growth) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-transfer-growth): Affine composition and dyadic expansion -> Operator norm growth
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-lem-positive-derivative-powers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-derivative-powers): Weighted area estimate and Hölder inequality -> Positive derivative-power integrability
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-disk-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-disk-transfer): Cell comparison and rotated arcs -> Uniform disk estimate conditional on transfer growth
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-weighted-law) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-weighted-law): Resolvent eigenmeasure and translation-scale averaging -> Weighted affine probability law
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-law-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-law-moments): Differentiation under expectation -> Logarithmic-derivative moments
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-proper-superlevels) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-escape-trace-2): Weighted inverse area, trace bounds, and target covers -> Compact positive superlevels almost surely
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-critical-pairing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-critical-pairing): Planar index count and equal-rank Borel assignment -> Injective equivariant saddle-to-maximum pairing
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-lem-root-exception) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-root-exception): Area formula and weighted affine covariance -> Vanishing exceptional positive weight at the root
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-prop-transfer-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-transfer-bound): Target-weight comparison and expanding-box exhaustion -> Transfer growth bound
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-derivative-powers-2): Disk comparison, Hölder interpolation, and conformal area change -> Sharp spectrum and open area-integrability range
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-cor-sobolev-pullback) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-sobolev-pullback): Sobolev composition equivalence -> Bounded pullback with map-dependent seminorm bounds

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-grid**: Every affine word has the stated dyadic height and coefficient, and each grid index occurs exactly once.
- **I-area**: The derivative area integral is finite throughout the stated open exponent range.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-endpoints**: Include either endpoint in the derivative integrability range. -> The Koebe inverse has divergent endpoint area integrals.
- **A-domain**: Apply the area assertion to arbitrary deformations or multiply connected domains. -> The hypotheses of the asserted integrability result have not been established.
- **A-finite**: Use finite grid enumeration as a certificate for the analytic transfer bound. -> The limiting operator growth rate and the whole normalized class are not decided by finite word enumeration.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The finite affine-word construction is explicit in the proof of the dyadic grid identity. Its exact enumeration checks the grid coordinates and coefficients; it does not decide the analytic operator norm or its limiting growth rate.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON integer n on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: nonnegative integer n; resource cap n <= 10.
# OUTPUTS: JSON rows [real coordinate, height, coefficient] as exact rational strings, one per affine word.
# TIME: O(2^n) exact rational operations; rational bit lengths O(n).
# SPACE: O(n*2^n) bits for the word frontier and serialized output.
# ARITHMETIC: exact rational arithmetic using fmpq.
# FAILURE: invalid input or exceeded resource cap exits nonzero.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq
n = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
if type(n) is not int or not 0 <= n <= 10:
    raise ValueError("invalid depth")
frontier = [(fmpq(0), fmpq(1), fmpq(1))]
for depth in range(n):
    frontier = [(x + y * fmpq(sign, 2), y / 2, c / 2)
                for x, y, c in frontier for sign in (-1, 1)]
print(json.dumps([[str(x), str(y), str(c)] for x, y, c in frontier]))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-domain** (hypothesis): The domain area assertion requires a conformal bijection from a simply connected plane domain onto the unit disk, with the stated boundary condition in the Riemann sphere. Blocked use: ['Apply the domain assertion to a nonconformal map, a multiply connected domain, or a map that is not onto the disk.']
- **L-endpoints** (counterexample_scope): The area-integrability interval is open. The Koebe example obstructs a universal extension to either endpoint. Blocked use: ['Include either endpoint in the universal area-integrability conclusion.']
- **L-normalization** (hypothesis): The uniform radial estimate is stated for the normalized schlicht class. General univalent maps require the source normalization and its derivative multiplier in the area assertion. Blocked use: ['Use the normalized radial constant for arbitrary unnormalized maps without accounting for scaling.']
- **L-loss** (approximation): The uniform radial bounds retain an arbitrary positive exponent loss. The sharp limiting exponent does not supply a loss-free uniform bound or endpoint area integrability. Blocked use: ['Drop the positive exponent loss from the radial bound.', 'Deduce endpoint area finiteness from the sharp radial exponent.']
- **L-spectrum-order** (hypothesis): The class spectrum takes the supremum after the limiting exponent of each map has been formed. Blocked use: ['Interchange the class supremum and boundary limit without an additional argument.']
- **L-law** (hypothesis): The weighted probability law is constructed under the contrary transfer-growth assumption; it is an input to the contradiction argument. Blocked use: ['Treat that law as an unconditional sampling model after the transfer bound has been proved.']
- **L-typical-targets** (hypothesis): Compactness of positive superlevels is asserted for almost every sampled map and almost every planar target. The root target requires a separate exceptional-target argument. Blocked use: ['Replace the almost-everywhere target assertion by an assertion for every target.', 'Apply the pairing at the normalized root solely from the generic target statement.']
- **L-pairing** (hypothesis): The critical-point pairing requires good targets, including compact positive superlevels and regularity for the critical map. Blocked use: ['Apply the saddle-to-maximum assignment to a singular target or a target with escaping positive superlevels.']
- **L-finite-pairing** (nonconstructive): Finiteness is proved above each positive level, rather than for the entire critical fiber. The measurable pairing is not supplied as a terminating procedure for finding all roots of an arbitrary analytic map. Blocked use: ['Treat a finite sampled critical list as the complete analytic fiber without a completeness certificate.']
- **L-constants** (nonconstructive): The main estimate asserts existence of a finite constant depending on the exponent loss, without giving a numerical value or a procedure for computing it. Blocked use: ['Use the existence assertion as a computable numerical error bound.']
- **L-sobolev** (hypothesis): The Sobolev conclusion bounds homogeneous gradient seminorms under the stated domain and exponent hypotheses. Its finite bounds may depend on the map and the exponents. Blocked use: ['Infer a uniform bound over arbitrary maps.', 'Infer a bound on the full inhomogeneous Sobolev norm from the gradient-seminorm assertion alone.']
- **L-finite-witness** (formal_scope): Exact checks of a finite dyadic grid establish only that finite construction; they do not certify the analytic transfer bound, the weighted law, or Brennan’s conjecture. Blocked use: ['Promote a finite affine-word computation into a certificate of the universal analytic theorem.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 072 (`lean/docs/072.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Brennan's conjecture and sharp inverse-square integral means](../../preprints/Brennans-conjecture-and-sharp-inverse-square-integral-means-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A strict inverse-first-power bound for univalent functions](../../preprints/A-strict-inverse-first-power-bound-for-univalent-functions-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Brennan's conjecture asserts that $|\phi'|^s$ is area-integrable for $4/3<s<4$ when $\phi$ conformally maps a simply connected plane domain with nontrivial spherical boundary onto the disk. The formalization establishes this interval, along with area integrability of $|f'|^t$ for univalent disk maps when $-2<t<2/3$.
>
> It also gives the uniform inverse-square radial-mean bound with exponent $-1-\varepsilon$ for every $\varepsilon>0$ and the spectrum value $B_{\mathcal S}(-2)=1$. The Koebe map and its inverse give divergence at all four boundary exponents.
>
> Kraetzer's proposed integral-means spectrum predicts $B_b(-1)=1/4$ for bounded univalent maps. The formalized result proves $B_b(-1)<1/4$, contradicting that prediction.
>
> The underlying estimate gives constants $0<\varepsilon<1/4$ and $C<\infty$ such that the normalized circular mean of $|f'|^{-1}$ is at most $C(1-r)^{-1/4+\varepsilon}$ for every normalized univalent disk map and $1/2\le r<1$. The bound requires neither bounded image nor boundary regularity, and no numerical value of $\varepsilon$ is specified.

Comparator source `Brennan.lean` (1715 bytes, sha256 `07ad707efc006317e91d7357ff390308e2c50402c636d0ffba2e87b88ffe570d`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open Set MeasureTheory Filter
open scoped Topology
namespace Brennan

def disk : Set ℂ := Metric.ball 0 1

def UnivalentOn (f : ℂ → ℂ) (U : Set ℂ) : Prop :=
  DifferentiableOn ℂ f U ∧ Set.InjOn f U

def Schlicht (f : ℂ → ℂ) : Prop :=
  UnivalentOn f disk ∧ f 0 = 0 ∧ deriv f 0 = 1

def integralMean (f : ℂ → ℂ) (t r : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  (2 * Real.pi)⁻¹ * ∫ θ in -Real.pi..Real.pi,
    ‖deriv f ((r : ℂ) * Complex.exp ((θ : ℂ) * Complex.I))‖ ^ t

def beta (f : ℂ → ℂ) (t : ℝ) : EReal :=
  Filter.limsup (fun r : ℝ =>
    ((Real.log (integralMean f t r) / Real.log (1 / (1 - r)) : ℝ) : EReal))
    (𝓝[<] (1 : ℝ))

def spectrum (t : ℝ) : EReal :=
  sSup {b | ∃ f : ℂ → ℂ, Schlicht f ∧ beta f t = b}

def SphericalBoundaryNontrivial (W : Set ℂ) : Prop :=
  Set.Nontrivial (frontier (((↑) : ℂ → OnePoint ℂ) '' W))

def MainStatement : Prop :=
  (∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ C : ℝ, ∀ f : ℂ → ℂ, Schlicht f →
    ∀ r : ℝ, 1 / 2 ≤ r → r < 1 →
      integralMean f (-2) r ≤ C * (1 - r) ^ (-1 - ε)) ∧
  spectrum (-2) = 1 ∧
  (∀ W : Set ℂ, IsOpen W → IsConnected W → SimplyConnectedSpace W →
    SphericalBoundaryNontrivial W → ∀ φ : ℂ → ℂ,
      DifferentiableOn ℂ φ W → Set.BijOn φ W disk →
      ∀ s : ℝ, 4 / 3 < s → s < 4 →
        IntegrableOn (fun z => ‖deriv φ z‖ ^ s) W volume) ∧
  (∀ f : ℂ → ℂ, UnivalentOn f disk → ∀ t : ℝ,
    -2 < t → t < 2 / 3 →
      IntegrableOn (fun z => ‖deriv f z‖ ^ t) disk volume)

theorem main_theorem : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end Brennan
end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove Brennan's conjecture: if a simply connected plane domain admits a conformal bijection <i>φ</i> onto the unit disk, then $`|\varphi'|^s`$ is area-integrable for every $`4/3\lt s\lt 4`$. We also prove the sharp inverse-square integral-means exponent $`B_{\mathcal S}(-2)=1`$ for the normalized schlicht class $`\mathcal S`$.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\mathbb D=\{z\in\mathbb C:|z|<1\}$, and write $dA=dx\,dy$ for planar Lebesgue measure. A domain is a connected open subset of $\mathbb C$; a univalent map is an injective holomorphic map. Brennan’s conjecture asks whether, for every conformal bijection $\varphi:W\to\mathbb D$ from a simply connected plane domain, the derivative satisfies $$\int_W|\varphi'(z)|^s\,dA(z)<\infty
 \qquad\left(\frac43<s<4\right).$$ There is no regularity assumption on the boundary and no boundedness assumption on $W$. The problem measures how strongly a conformal map can compress area near an arbitrary boundary. The conjecture dates to Brennan’s 1978 work (Brennan 1978b) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Brennan1978](#ref-Brennan1978)]; the domain and integral-means formulations are recorded in (Bertilsson 1999, chap. 3) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Bertilsson1999](#ref-Bertilsson1999)].

The inverse map $f=\varphi^{-1}:\mathbb D\to W$ puts all source domains on the same footing. Conformal change of variables gives <a id="eq:inverse-area"></a>$$\label{eq:inverse-area}
 \int_W|\varphi'|^s\,dA
 =\int_{\mathbb D}|f'|^{2-s}\,dA.$$ Thus the conjectured interval becomes $-2<t<2/3$, with $t=2-s$. Positive powers detect large derivatives, whereas the difficult negative powers detect small derivatives. The inverse-square power $t=-2$ is the limiting case relevant to the upper endpoint $s=4$.

To state its radial form, let $$\mathcal S=\{f:\mathbb D\to\mathbb C\text{ univalent}:f(0)=0,\ f'(0)=1\}$$ be the normalized schlicht class. For $f\in\mathcal S$, $t\in\mathbb R$, and $0<r<1$, define $$M_t[f'](r)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
                  |f'(re^{i\theta})|^t\,d\theta,$$ and put $$\begin{aligned}
 \beta_f(t)&=\limsup_{r\uparrow1}
       \frac{\log M_t[f'](r)}{\log(1/(1-r))},\\
 B_{\mathcal S}(t)&=\sup_{f\in\mathcal S}\beta_f(t).
\end{aligned}$$ Equivalently, $\beta_f(t)$ is the infimum of the real $b$ for which $M_t[f'](r)=O((1-r)^{-b})$ as $r\uparrow1$; the implicit constant may depend on $f$. In particular, the supremum defining $B_{\mathcal S}$ is taken after forming each map’s limiting exponent. We prove the following uniform estimate directly, and then derive the area assertion.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main-uniform"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Brennan’s conjecture and inverse-square means). *For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that $$\label{eq:main-uniform}
 M_{-2}[f'](r)\le C_\varepsilon(1-r)^{-1-\varepsilon}
 \qquad\left(f\in\mathcal S,\ \frac12\le r<1\right).$$ Moreover, $B_{\mathcal S}(-2)=1$.*

*Let $W\subset\mathbb C$ be any simply connected domain whose boundary in the Riemann sphere contains at least two points. Every conformal bijection $\varphi:W\to\mathbb D$ satisfies $$\int_W|\varphi'(z)|^s\,dA(z)<\infty
 \qquad\left(\frac43<s<4\right).$$ Equivalently, every univalent map $f:\mathbb D\to\mathbb C$ satisfies $$\int_{\mathbb D}|f'(w)|^t\,dA(w)<\infty
 \qquad\left(-2<t<\frac23\right).$$*

</div>

The domain correspondence, including the boundary condition, is justified in Section <a href="#sec:integrability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:integrability">7</a>. That section also derives $B_{\mathcal S}(t)=|t|-1$ for every $t\le-2$; the corresponding uniform radial bounds retain an arbitrary positive loss. The radial exponent $1$ is compatible with divergence of the area integral at $t=-2$. The other area endpoint also fails for the same classical example.

<a id="stmt-cor-sharp-endpoints"></a>
<div id="cor:sharp-endpoints" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Sharpness of the open range). *The Koebe function $f(z)=z/(1-z)^2$ belongs to $\mathcal S$ and satisfies $$\int_{\mathbb D}|f'|^{-2}\,dA=\infty,
 \qquad
 \int_{\mathbb D}|f'|^{2/3}\,dA=\infty.$$ Its inverse therefore fails area integrability at both $s=4$ and $s=4/3$.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and its development

Brennan’s 1978 work (Brennan 1978b, 1978a) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Brennan1978](#ref-Brennan1978), [Brennan1978LOMI](#ref-Brennan1978LOMI)] improved the previously known upper range beyond $s=3$, as recorded in Bertilsson’s historical account (Bertilsson 1999, chap. 3, pp. 43–44) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Bertilsson1999](#ref-Bertilsson1999)]. The standard formulation asks for the full interval $4/3<s<4$ on every simply connected plane domain admitting a conformal bijection to the disk. Neither boundedness nor boundary regularity is imposed. The classical slit-plane obstructions at both endpoints are verified in Section <a href="#sec:integrability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:integrability">7</a>.

Subsequent progress connected the area question to finer estimates for derivative integral means. Pommerenke developed this approach in (Pommerenke 1985a, 1985b) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Pommerenke1985IntegralMeans](#ref-Pommerenke1985IntegralMeans), [Pommerenke1985IntegralMeansII](#ref-Pommerenke1985IntegralMeansII)]. Carleson and Makarov related negative integral means to the distribution of large harmonic measure and proved the linear spectral tail $B_{\mathcal S}(t)=|t|-1$ for all sufficiently negative $t$ (Carleson and Makarov 1994, sec. 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[CarlesonMakarov1994](#ref-CarlesonMakarov1994)]. The proposed threshold $t=-2$ is the one associated with Brennan’s conjecture (Hedenmalm and Shimorin 2005) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[HedenmalmShimorin2005](#ref-HedenmalmShimorin2005)]. The statement for sufficiently negative parameters alone does not reach that endpoint.

Bertilsson studied coefficient estimates for negative powers of derivatives of univalent functions (Bertilsson 1998) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Bertilsson1998](#ref-Bertilsson1998)]. His thesis reports the area range $4/3<s<3.421$ (Bertilsson 1999) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Bertilsson1999](#ref-Bertilsson1999)] and establishes equivalences between the spectral endpoint and uniform integral-means formulations (Bertilsson 1999, Theorem 3.7, especially parts (a) and (f), pp. 44–45) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Bertilsson1999](#ref-Bertilsson1999)]. Thus uniform estimates have an established place in the formulation of Brennan’s problem. In the present argument, the common constant in <a href="#eq:main-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-uniform">[eq:main-uniform]</a> arises directly from an operator on the entire compact normalized class.

Hedenmalm and Shimorin combined area inequalities with weighted Bergman space methods to improve general bounds for the integral-means spectrum. Their published table gives $B_{\mathcal S}(-2)\le1.218$ (Hedenmalm and Shimorin 2005, Table 1, p. 385) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HedenmalmShimorin2005](#ref-HedenmalmShimorin2005)]. Sola extended the Bergman-space computation (Sola 2006) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Sola2006](#ref-Sola2006)], reporting the improved estimate $B_{\mathcal S}(-2)\le1.206$ in his thesis (Sola 2007, 6) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Sola2007Thesis](#ref-Sola2007Thesis)]. These estimates illustrate the remaining gap between general analytic bounds and the conjectured exponent $1$.

Recent works treat the conjectured integrability range under additional hypotheses. Aleman and Kouroupis prove it for members of continuous semigroups of conformal disk self-maps and for Koenigs maps of nontrivial such semigroups (Aleman and Kouroupis 2025, Theorem 1.1 and Proposition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[AlemanKouroupis2025](#ref-AlemanKouroupis2025)]. Zheng treats simply connected polynomial basins at infinity for $P(z)=z^m+a_0$, $m\ge3$, and for monic degree-$m$ polynomials whose coefficients of $z,\ldots,z^{m-1}$ have modulus smaller than a degree-dependent threshold; these basins are considered in the sphere and contain infinity (Zheng 2026, Theorems 1.3–1.4) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Zheng2026Basins](#ref-Zheng2026Basins)]. Jin computes the real integral-means spectra of normalized rational functions univalent in the disk, with inverse-square supremum $1$ over that class (Jin 2025, Theorem 6.20 and Corollaries 6.22–6.23) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Jin2025IntegralMeans](#ref-Jin2025IntegralMeans)]. His work on Schwarzian multiplication operators recalls Shimorin’s sufficient multiplier-norm criterion for the inverse-square endpoint of an individual normalized univalent function (Jin 2026, Proposition 1.4(2)) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Jin2026Multipliers](#ref-Jin2026Multipliers)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> concerns the full class of univalent disk maps.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Analytic and geometric ingredients

Normalized changes of base point, distortion estimates, and compactness of the schlicht class are classical tools of geometric function theory (Duren 1983, vol. 259, chaps. 1–2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Duren1983](#ref-Duren1983)]. A related probabilistic method for exterior integral means appears in the conformal snowflakes of Beliaev and Smirnov. Their positive integral operator gives lower bounds for an averaged exponent defined from expected derivative integral means. For each real $t$ and $\varepsilon>0$, they construct a random snowflake whose averaged exponent exceeds $B(t)-\varepsilon$, where $B$ is their universal spectrum defined using exterior Riemann maps (Beliaev and Smirnov 2010, Lemma 3.4 and Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BeliaevSmirnov2010](#ref-BeliaevSmirnov2010)]. Here the operator acts on all normalized half-plane maps, and hypothetical excess growth produces a weighted affine law. The operator is defined in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>; Section <a href="#sec:weighted-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted-law">3</a> constructs its eigenmeasure and proves the averaging identity.

The critical-point comparison is a planar index argument. Its boundary calculation uses Hopf’s secant proof of the tangent-turning theorem (Hopf 1935, sec. 2, pp. 53–55) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Hopf1935](#ref-Hopf1935)], while the regular-target step is an elementary plane case of Sard’s theorem (Sard 1942, Theorem 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Sard1942](#ref-Sard1942)]. We keep the index of a possible pole separate from those of the finite critical points. The pairing is defined by ranks in ordered lists; its measurability is proved through continuous local inverse branches, and its equivariance follows from the ordering itself.

The subsequent area comparison is related in form to equivariant mass transport, including the general framework for nonunimodular group actions developed by Gentner and Last (Gentner and Last 2011) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[GentnerLast2011](#ref-GentnerLast2011)]. We use a direct calculation with local changes of variables and explicit expanding boxes. The affine covariance, exceptional-target transfer, and boundary-cost estimates needed here are all proved within the paper.

An independent companion applies a related affine-law and critical-point comparison to prove a uniform inverse-first-power bound with exponent strictly below $1/4$ for normalized univalent disk maps, disproving Kraetzer’s bounded-spectrum prediction at $p=-1$ (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[OpenAIStrictInverseFirst2026](#ref-OpenAIStrictInverseFirst2026)]. The proof here does not use that strict estimate; its Hölder interpolation from <a href="#eq:main-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-uniform">[eq:main-uniform]</a> gives exponent $1/2$ with an arbitrary positive loss at $t=-1$.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The obstacle and the proof strategy

The classical distortion theorem bounds $|f'(z)|^{-1}$ by a constant times $(1-|z|)^{-1}$ for $f\in\mathcal S$. Squaring this estimate gives radial growth with exponent $2$, while the required exponent is $1$. The missing information concerns how often very small derivatives can occur around a circle. Our proof encodes hypothetical excess growth in a probability law on conformal maps. That law imposes an algebraic sign on an expected Jacobian; a geometric pairing of critical points imposes the opposite sign.

We work on the upper half-plane $\mathbb H=\{x+iy:y>0\}$ with the compact class $\mathcal K$ of univalent maps $F$ normalized by $F(i)=0$ and $F'(i)=1$. Write $Q_F=1/F'$. Changing the base point to $z=x+iy$ gives $$T_zF(w)=\frac{F(x+yw)-F(x+iy)}{yF'(x+iy)}.$$ Each $T_zF$ again belongs to $\mathcal K$. A positive operator averages the two changes of base point $z=\pm1/2+i/2$, with weights $|Q_F(z)|^2$. Its iterates sample equally spaced points above a fixed interval at dyadic heights. If $\rho$ denotes the exponential growth rate of their operator norms, Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> shows that $\rho\le2$ implies the uniform disk estimate in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

Suppose instead that $\rho>2$, and put $\beta=\log_2\rho>1$. Section <a href="#sec:weighted-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:weighted-law">3</a> constructs a Borel probability law $P$ on $\mathcal K$ with the following property: for every $x+iy\in\mathbb H$ and every nonnegative Borel function $\Phi$ on $\mathcal K$, $$\mathbb E_P\bigl[|Q_F(x+iy)|^2\Phi(T_{x+iy}F)\bigr]
       =y^{-\beta}\mathbb E_P\Phi(F).$$ We first construct a positive eigenmeasure, then average horizontal translations and logarithmic scales. The resulting identity holds at every affine change of base point. Differentiating the constant-test identity determines the moments of the logarithmic derivative that will control the Jacobian.

For $\xi\in\mathbb C$, consider the target potential $$V_\xi^F(x+iy)=\frac{y^k}{|F(x+iy)-\xi|},
 \qquad k=\frac{\beta+3}{4}>1.$$ At the unique possible preimage of $\xi$ we give it value $+\infty$. A critical-point count is useful only if points above a fixed positive level cannot escape to the real axis or to infinity. Section <a href="#sec:escape" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:escape">4</a> proves precisely this compactness for almost every target and almost every sampled map. The main boundary estimate controls the total length of a horizontal line mapped into a compact target set.

The critical points of $V_\xi^F$ solve $$G_F(z)=\xi,\qquad G_F(x+iy)=F(x+iy)-\frac{iy}{k}F'(x+iy).$$ Section <a href="#sec:critical-pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:critical-pairing">5</a> shows that the sign of the normalized real Jacobian of $G_F$ distinguishes saddles from maxima when $\xi$ is a regular value. For these targets, an index count on compact superlevel boundaries gives at least as many maxima as saddles above each positive level. Ordering the two lists by their values of $V_\xi^F$ therefore pairs each saddle with a distinct maximum of at least the same value. We prove that this assignment is Borel and respects every affine change of source coordinates. These properties are essential when the pointwise pairing is integrated against $P$.

Section <a href="#sec:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:transport">6</a> performs that integration. Change of variables through $G_F$ converts a weighted source area into the target weight $(V_\xi^F/k)^4$. The pairing increases this weight. Affine covariance then turns the expected source density into $dx\,dy/y$; expanding boxes have asymptotically negligible boundary enlargement. This proves that the expected normalized Jacobian is nonpositive, contradicting its strictly positive value from the logarithmic-derivative moments. A separate application of the same area formula handles the exceptional targets at the distinguished point $i$, whose target $G_F(i)=-i/k$ is fixed by normalization.

Finally, Section <a href="#sec:integrability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:integrability">7</a> obtains the negative area exponents $-2<t<0$ by Hölder’s inequality and treats the positive exponents with classical growth estimates and a weighted area argument. It also proves the equivalence with the endpoint spectrum and the Koebe sharpness assertions. Thus the analytic law, the compactness of superlevels, and the measurable pairing each supply a distinct input to the same weighted area contradiction.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Univalent maps and a dyadic transfer operator

We first place normalized univalent maps of the upper half-plane in a compact space. Rescaling the source about a point then defines a positive operator weighted by the inverse square of the derivative. The main conclusion of this section is a uniform comparison between powers of that operator and disk integral means. Later sections will bound the operator’s growth rate.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Classical estimates and the normalized compact class

We use the classical coefficient, distortion, growth, and quarter-disk estimates in the following precise forms; see Duren (Duren 1983, vol. 259, chap. 2, especially §2.3) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Duren1983](#ref-Duren1983)]. No boundary regularity or boundedness of the image is required.

<a id="stmt-lem-classical-univalence"></a>
<div id="lem:classical-univalence" class="lemma">

<a id="prelim:disk-bounds"></a>
<a id="prelim:quarter"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Classical univalence estimates). *If $f(z)=z+a_2z^2+\cdots$ belongs to $\mathcal S$, then $|a_2|\le2$ and, for $|z|=r<1$, $$\frac{1-r}{(1+r)^3}\le |f'(z)|\le\frac{1+r}{(1-r)^3},
 \qquad \frac r4\le |f(z)|\le\frac{r}{(1-r)^2}.
 \label{prelim:disk-bounds}$$ Every univalent holomorphic map $g$ on an open disk $B(z_0,R)$ satisfies $$B\bigl(g(z_0),R|g'(z_0)|/4\bigr)\subset g(B(z_0,R)).
 \label{prelim:quarter}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-classical-univalence"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-classical-univalence</summary>

*Proof.* The coefficient and derivative bounds are the classical estimates just cited. The classical lower growth bound $r/(1+r)^2$ implies the weaker bound $r/4$ recorded here. A univalent holomorphic map has nonzero derivative, so for the last assertion the map $$\zeta\longmapsto
 \frac{g(z_0+R\zeta)-g(z_0)}{R g'(z_0)}$$ belongs to $\mathcal S$. Applying the Koebe quarter theorem to this normalized map gives <a href="#prelim:quarter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:quarter">[prelim:quarter]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="prelim:normalization"></a>
Write $$\mathbb H=\{x+iy:x\in\mathbb R,\ y>0\},\qquad
 m(z)=\frac{z-i}{z+i},$$ and let $$\mathcal K=\{F:\mathbb H\to\mathbb C\text{ univalent}:F(i)=0,\ F'(i)=1\}.$$ We give $\mathcal K$ the topology of locally uniform convergence. Since $m'(i)=1/(2i)$, its correspondence with the disk class is exactly $$F(z)=2i f(m(z)),\qquad
 f(\zeta)=\frac{F(m^{-1}(\zeta))}{2i}.
 \label{prelim:normalization}$$ For $F\in\mathcal K$, define the holomorphic function $$Q_F(z)=\frac1{F'(z)}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-compact-class"></a>
<div id="lem:compact-class" class="lemma">

<a id="prelim:strip-bound"></a>
<a id="prelim:zero-separation"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Compactness and uniform local bounds). *The space $\mathcal K$ is compact and metrizable. For every integer $j\ge0$, the evaluations $(F,z)\mapsto F^{(j)}(z)$ and $(F,z)\mapsto Q_F^{(j)}(z)$ are jointly continuous on $\mathcal K\times\mathbb H$ and bounded on $\mathcal K\times E$ for every compact $E\subset\mathbb H$. For every $C_0\ge0$, $$|Q_F(x+iy)|\le\frac{(C_0^2+4)^2}{y}
 \quad\bigl(F\in\mathcal K,\ |x|\le C_0,\ 0<y\le1\bigr).
 \label{prelim:strip-bound}$$ In particular the bound is $64/y$ when $|x|\le2$. Also, $$|F(z)|\ge\frac1{16}
 \quad\text{if }F\in\mathcal K\text{ and }|m(z)|\ge\frac12.
 \label{prelim:zero-separation}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compact-class"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compact-class</summary>

*Proof.* The upper growth bound and <a href="#prelim:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:normalization">[prelim:normalization]</a> give local uniform boundedness. Montel’s theorem therefore gives a locally uniformly convergent subsequence from every sequence in $\mathcal K$; see (Duren 1983, vol. 259, sec. 1.1 and 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Duren1983](#ref-Duren1983)] for this normal-family argument and the univalent-limit form of Hurwitz’s theorem. Cauchy’s formula preserves the derivative normalization, so a limit $F$ has $F'(i)=1$ and is nonconstant. For any fixed $z_0\in\mathbb H$, apply Hurwitz’s theorem to $F_n(z)-F_n(z_0)$ on the connected domain $\mathbb H\setminus\{z_0\}$. The limit is not identically zero and has no zero there. Thus $F$ is injective and belongs to $\mathcal K$. The compact-open topology is metrizable using a countable compact exhaustion of $\mathbb H$, so this sequential compactness proves compactness.

Cauchy’s formula gives locally uniform convergence of all derivatives. The limiting derivative is nonzero, so reciprocals also converge locally uniformly, as do their derivatives. These observations give the asserted joint continuity. Compactness of $\mathcal K\times E$ then gives the uniform bounds.

For the explicit estimate, put $D=|x+i(y+1)|^2$ and $r=|m(x+iy)|$. Then $$1-r^2=\frac{4y}{D},\qquad
 |Q_F(x+iy)|=\frac{D}{4|f'(m(x+iy))|}.$$ The lower derivative bound in <a href="#prelim:disk-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:disk-bounds">[prelim:disk-bounds]</a> gives $$|Q_F(x+iy)|
 \le\frac{D(1+r)^3}{4(1-r)}
 =\frac{D^2(1+r)^4}{16y}
 \le\frac{D^2}{y}.$$ When $|x|\le C_0$ and $y\le1$, this proves <a href="#prelim:strip-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:strip-bound">[prelim:strip-bound]</a>.

Finally $|m(z)|<1/7$ on $B(i,1/4)$, while <a href="#prelim:quarter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:quarter">[prelim:quarter]</a> gives $B(0,1/16)\subset F(B(i,1/4))$. Injectivity prevents any point outside $B(i,1/4)$ from having an image of modulus less than $1/16$. This proves <a href="#prelim:zero-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:zero-separation">[prelim:zero-separation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Affine rescaling and dyadic growth

<a id="prelim:rerooting"></a>
<a id="prelim:operators"></a>
Identify $z=x+iy\in\mathbb H$ with the affine map $w\mapsto x+yw$. Composition of these source maps is the product <a id="prelim:affine-product"></a>$$z\cdot w=x+yw,
 \qquad z^\#=\frac{-x+i}{y},
 \label{prelim:affine-product}$$ with identity $i$ and inverse $z^\#$. For $F\in\mathcal K$ define $$T_zF(w)=\frac{F(z\cdot w)-F(z)}{yF'(z)}.
 \label{prelim:rerooting}$$ It belongs to $\mathcal K$. On the space $C(\mathcal K)$ of continuous complex-valued functions, equipped with its supremum norm, define $$A_z\Phi(F)=|Q_F(z)|^2\Phi(T_zF),\qquad
 L=\frac12\bigl(A_{-1/2+i/2}+A_{1/2+i/2}\bigr).
 \label{prelim:operators}$$ An operator is called positive here if it takes nonnegative real-valued functions to nonnegative functions. The operators in <a href="#prelim:operators" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:operators">[prelim:operators]</a> are positive. Their weights measure how the inverse derivative changes when the source is rescaled.

<a id="stmt-lem-transfer-growth"></a>
<div id="lem:transfer-growth" class="lemma">

<a id="prelim:composition"></a>
<a id="prelim:rho"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Affine identities and transfer growth). *For $z,w\in\mathbb H$ and $F\in\mathcal K$, $$Q_{T_zF}(w)=\frac{Q_F(z\cdot w)}{Q_F(z)},\qquad
 T_w(T_zF)=T_{z\cdot w}F,\qquad A_zA_w=A_{z\cdot w}.
 \label{prelim:composition}$$ Each $A_z$ is bounded on $C(\mathcal K)$, and for fixed $\Phi\in C(\mathcal K)$ the map $z\mapsto A_z\Phi$ is continuous in the supremum norm. For $n\ge0$, put $v_n=2^{-n}$ and $u_{n,j}=-1+(2j+1)v_n$, $0\le j<2^n$. Then <a id="prelim:dyadic-grid"></a>$$L^n=v_n\sum_{j=0}^{2^n-1} A_{u_{n,j}+iv_n},\qquad
 \|L^n\|=\max_{F\in\mathcal K}L^n\mathbf1(F).
 \label{prelim:dyadic-grid}$$ The limit $$\rho=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|L^n\|^{1/n}
      =\inf_{n\ge1}\|L^n\|^{1/n}
 \label{prelim:rho}$$ exists and satisfies $1\le\rho\le4$. If $\lambda>\rho$, there is $C_\lambda<\infty$ such that $\|L^n\|\le C_\lambda\lambda^n$ for all $n\ge0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transfer-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transfer-growth</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiating <a href="#prelim:rerooting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:rerooting">[prelim:rerooting]</a> proves the first identity in <a href="#prelim:composition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:composition">[prelim:composition]</a>. Applying the normalization twice proves the second. In the operator product the weights telescope: $$|Q_F(z)|^2|Q_{T_zF}(w)|^2=|Q_F(z\cdot w)|^2.$$ This gives the third identity, with its displayed order of multiplication. Joint continuity of $(z,F)\mapsto T_zF$ follows from locally uniform convergence on affine images of compact source sets and the nonzero normalizing denominator. Together with Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-class">4</a>, this proves boundedness of $A_z$. For fixed $\Phi$, joint continuity on $\mathcal K$ times a compact neighborhood of $z$ gives the asserted supremum-norm continuity.

A word of length $n$ in the affine maps $-1/2+i/2$ and $1/2+i/2$ has height $2^{-n}$ and real part $\sum_{l=1}^n\sigma_l2^{-l}$, where $\sigma_l\in\{-1,1\}$. Writing $\sigma_l=2\epsilon_l-1$ and $j=\sum_{l=1}^n\epsilon_l2^{n-l}$ gives real part $-1+(2j+1)2^{-n}$. Each $j$ occurs once and each word has coefficient $2^{-n}$. Expansion of $L^n$ proves the grid formula; at $n=0$ it reduces to $A_i=I$.

For the positive sums defining $L^n$, $$|L^n\Phi|\le L^n|\Phi|\le\|\Phi\|_\infty L^n\mathbf1.$$ Testing with $\Phi=\mathbf1$ proves the norm identity. At the map $F(z)=z-i$ every summand in $L^n\mathbf1(F)$ equals one, so $\|L^n\|\ge1$. Since $|u_{n,j}|\le1$, <a href="#prelim:strip-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:strip-bound">[prelim:strip-bound]</a> gives $$\|L^n\|\le625\,4^n.$$ The logarithms of these positive norms are subadditive. More explicitly, for fixed $N\ge1$ write $n=qN+r$, $0\le r<N$, and use $\|L^n\|\le\|L^N\|^q\|L^r\|$. Taking the upper limit of the $n$th roots and then the infimum over $N$ proves <a href="#prelim:rho" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:rho">[prelim:rho]</a>; the displayed lower and upper bounds give $1\le\rho\le4$. If $\lambda>\rho$, the root limit bounds $\|L^n\|$ by $\lambda^n$ for all sufficiently large $n$. Increasing a constant to cover the finitely many remaining $n$ proves the last claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### From dyadic samples to disk integral means

The next proposition explains why an upper bound on $\rho$ controls an entire circle. The comparison uses the same compact family in every cell, so all its constants are independent of the chosen univalent map.

<a id="stmt-prop-disk-transfer"></a>
<div id="prop:disk-transfer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Uniform disk comparison). *If $\rho\le2$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that <a id="prelim:uniform-disk"></a>$$M_{-2}[f'](r)\le C_\varepsilon(1-r)^{-1-\varepsilon}
 \quad\bigl(f\in\mathcal S,\ 1/2\le r<1\bigr).
 \label{prelim:uniform-disk}$$ The constant depends only on $\varepsilon$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-disk-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-disk-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $f\in\mathcal S$ and $1/2\le r<1$. Put $\delta=1-r$ and choose $n\ge1$ with $v=2^{-n}\le\delta<2v$. On the fixed angular interval $I=[3\pi/4,5\pi/4]$, write $$m^{-1}(re^{i\theta})=x(\theta)+iy(\theta),\qquad
 D(\theta)=1-2r\cos\theta+r^2.$$ The inverse formula gives <a id="prelim:arc-coordinates"></a>$$x(\theta)=\frac{-2r\sin\theta}{D(\theta)},\qquad
 y(\theta)=\frac{1-r^2}{D(\theta)}.
 \label{prelim:arc-coordinates}$$ On $I$ we have $\cos\theta\le0$ and hence $D\ge1+r^2\ge2r$; therefore $|x|\le|\sin\theta|<1$. Also $$\frac\delta2\le y\le2\delta,\qquad
 \frac{dx}{d\theta}
 =\frac{4r^2-2r(1+r^2)\cos\theta}{D(\theta)^2}
 \ge\frac1{16}.$$ The height bounds use $D\le(1+r)^2$, $D\ge1$, and $1+r\le2$. For the derivative bound use $4r^2\ge1$ and $D^2\le16$.

<a id="prelim:cell-comparison"></a>
The cells $[u_{n,j}-v,u_{n,j}+v]$ cover $[-1,1]$ with disjoint interiors. If $x(\theta)$ belongs to cell $j$, then $$w=\frac{x(\theta)-u_{n,j}}v+i\frac{y(\theta)}v
 \in E:=\{u+i\eta:|u|\le1,\ 1/2\le\eta\le4\}.$$ Let $B_E=\max_{G\in\mathcal K,\,w\in E}|Q_G(w)|$, which is finite by Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-class">4</a>. The first identity in <a href="#prelim:composition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:composition">[prelim:composition]</a> gives $$|Q_F(x(\theta)+iy(\theta))|
 \le B_E|Q_F(u_{n,j}+iv)|,
 \qquad F=2i f\circ m.
 \label{prelim:cell-comparison}$$ Thus the comparison covers every point of the varying-height arc in each cell.

The exact derivative conversion in <a href="#prelim:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:normalization">[prelim:normalization]</a> is $$|f'(m(z))|^{-2}
 =\left|\frac{m'(z)}{m'(i)}\right|^2|Q_F(z)|^2
 =\frac{16}{|z+i|^4}|Q_F(z)|^2
 \le16|Q_F(z)|^2.$$ Since $x(\theta)$ is increasing and $d\theta\le16\,dx$, <a href="#prelim:cell-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="prelim:cell-comparison">[prelim:cell-comparison]</a> and the cell lengths $2v$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_I|f'(re^{i\theta})|^{-2}\,d\theta
 &\le256B_E^2\sum_j|Q_F(u_{n,j}+iv)|^2
       \bigl|[u_{n,j}-v,u_{n,j}+v]\cap x(I)\bigr|\\
 &\le512B_E^2 v\sum_j|Q_F(u_{n,j}+iv)|^2
 =512B_E^2 L^n\mathbf1(F).
\end{aligned}$$ Here the bars around the intersection denote interval length.

For $\alpha\in\{1,i,-1,-i\}$, the map $f_\alpha(z)=\alpha^{-1}f(\alpha z)$ belongs to $\mathcal S$ and has derivative $f'(\alpha z)$. The four rotated copies of $I$ cover the full circle, with overlap only at endpoints. Applying the preceding estimate to these four maps therefore proves $$M_{-2}[f'](r)\le C\|L^n\|$$ with an absolute constant $C$. If $\rho\le2$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transfer-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transfer-growth">5</a> with $\lambda=2^{1+\varepsilon}$. It gives $$M_{-2}[f'](r)\le C C_\lambda v^{-1-\varepsilon}
 \le C C_\lambda 2^{1+\varepsilon}(1-r)^{-1-\varepsilon}.$$ The operator $L$ and the compact set $E$ were fixed before $f$ and $r$ were chosen, so this constant depends only on $\varepsilon$. ◻

</details>

We have reduced the uniform disk estimate to $\rho\le2$. The next sections assume $\rho>2$ and derive a contradiction from the probability law generated by the same positive operator.

<a id="sec:weighted-law"></a>
### A probability law from transfer growth

The disk estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:disk-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:disk-transfer">6</a> reduces our task to proving that $\rho\leq 2$. We now suppose that $\rho>2$ and construct a probability measure on the normalized family $\mathcal K$. Its covariance under affine changes of coordinates will turn geometric quantities into expectations at the single point $i$.

We use the operators $A_z$ and $L$ from the preceding section. Measures act on their left: if $\mu$ is a finite measure, then $(\mu A_z)(\Phi)=\mu(A_z\Phi)$ for $\Phi\in C(\mathcal K)$. Every positive bounded functional on $C(\mathcal K)$ is identified with its finite Borel measure by the Riesz representation theorem (Folland 1999, Theorem 7.2) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Folland1999](#ref-Folland1999)].

<a id="stmt-lem-weighted-law"></a>
<div id="lem:weighted-law" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:weighted-covariance"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Weighted affine covariance). *Suppose that $\rho>2$, and put $\beta=\log_2\rho>1$. There is a Borel probability measure $P$ on $\mathcal K$ such that, for every $z=x+iy\in\mathbb H$ and every nonnegative Borel function $\Phi:\mathcal K\to[0,\infty]$, $$\mathbb E\!\left[|Q_F(z)|^2\Phi(T_zF)\right]
   =y^{-\beta}\mathbb E\Phi(F),
 \label{eq:weighted-covariance}$$ where $\mathbb E$ denotes expectation with respect to $P$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weighted-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-law</summary>

*Proof.* We first obtain a probability eigenmeasure for $L$. The dyadic formula for $L^n$ will then give one horizontal period. Averaging over that period and over one logarithmic scale interval produces <a href="#eq:weighted-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-covariance">[eq:weighted-covariance]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### The eigenmeasure.

<a id="eq:resolvent-divergence"></a>
For $\tau>\rho$, the root limit defining $\rho$ implies that $$R_\tau=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\tau^{-n}L^n$$ converges in operator norm. Set $$g_\tau=R_\tau\mathbf 1,
 \qquad M_\tau=\max_{F\in\mathcal K}g_\tau(F).$$ Positivity gives $1\leq g_\tau\leq M_\tau$, and the maximum is attained because $g_\tau$ is continuous and $\mathcal K$ is compact. We claim that $$M_\tau\longrightarrow\infty\qquad(\tau\downarrow\rho).
 \label{eq:resolvent-divergence}$$ Indeed, $$Lg_\tau=\tau(g_\tau-\mathbf 1)
 \leq\tau(1-M_\tau^{-1})g_\tau.$$ Iteration, using positivity and $\mathbf 1\leq g_\tau$, gives $$L^n\mathbf 1
 \leq\bigl[\tau(1-M_\tau^{-1})\bigr]^n g_\tau.$$ Since $\|L^n\|=\|L^n\mathbf 1\|_\infty$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:transfer-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transfer-growth">5</a>, it follows that $$\|L^n\|\leq M_\tau
      \bigl[\tau(1-M_\tau^{-1})\bigr]^n.$$ If $M_{\tau_j}\leq M<\infty$ along a sequence $\tau_j\downarrow\rho$, taking the $n$th-root limit for each fixed $j$ would give $$\rho\leq\tau_j(1-M_{\tau_j}^{-1})
       \leq\tau_j(1-M^{-1}).$$ Letting $j\to\infty$ contradicts $\rho>0$, proving <a href="#eq:resolvent-divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:resolvent-divergence">[eq:resolvent-divergence]</a>.

<a id="eq:transfer-eigenmeasure"></a>
Choose a maximizer $F_\tau$ and define $$\mu_\tau=\frac{\delta_{F_\tau}R_\tau}{M_\tau}.$$ This is a positive functional of mass one, hence a Borel probability measure. The norm-convergent series gives $R_\tau L=\tau(R_\tau-I)$, where $I$ is the identity operator, so $$\mu_\tau L=\tau\mu_\tau-
                \frac{\tau}{M_\tau}\delta_{F_\tau}.$$ Choose a sequence $\tau\downarrow\rho$. Since $\mathcal K$ is compact metric, $C(\mathcal K)$ has a countable uniformly dense subset. Diagonal selection gives a subsequence along which the integrals of those functions converge. The bound $\|\mu_\tau\|=1$ extends convergence to every function in $C(\mathcal K)$. The limiting functional is positive and has mass one, hence is a probability measure $\mu$ by Riesz representation. For each $\Phi\in C(\mathcal K)$, the function $L\Phi$ is continuous, and the final term above has absolute value at most $\tau\|\Phi\|_\infty/M_\tau$. Consequently $$\mu L=\rho\mu.
 \label{eq:transfer-eigenmeasure}$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### A horizontal period.

For $v=2^{-n}$ and $u_j=-1+(2j+1)v$, the formula from Lemma <a href="#lem:transfer-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transfer-growth">5</a> reads $$L^n=v\sum_{j=0}^{2^n-1}A_{u_j+iv}.$$ Right composition with $A_{2+i}$ replaces $u_j$ by $u_j+2v$. Thus <a href="#eq:transfer-eigenmeasure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-eigenmeasure">[eq:transfer-eigenmeasure]</a> gives the exact cancellation <a id="eq:horizontal-endpoints"></a>$$\mu A_{2+i}-\mu
 =\rho^{-n}v\mu
    \bigl(A_{1+v+iv}-A_{-1+v+iv}\bigr).
 \label{eq:horizontal-endpoints}$$ Both remaining abscissae have absolute value at most $2$. The bound $|Q_F(u+iv)|\leq C/v$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-class">4</a> therefore implies $$\|\mu A_{2+i}-\mu\|
 \leq C\rho^{-n}v\,v^{-2}
 =C(2/\rho)^n\longrightarrow0.$$ The left side is independent of $n$, so $\mu A_{2+i}=\mu$. Composition with $A_{-2+i}$ gives the negative period as well.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Horizontal and scale averages.

<a id="eq:horizontal-average"></a>
Define, by integration on continuous test functions, $$\bar\mu=\frac12\int_0^2\mu A_{x+i}\,dx.$$ For each continuous test, the integrand is continuous in $x$, by joint continuity of the normalized transformations and their weights. Compactness of the parameter interval makes this a finite positive functional, and its mass is strictly positive because the weights $|Q_F(x+i)|^2$ are positive. The period just proved shows that $$\bar\mu A_{t+i}=\bar\mu\qquad(t\in\mathbb R).
 \label{eq:horizontal-average}$$

We next compute the change under a factor of two in height. Using <a href="#eq:transfer-eigenmeasure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transfer-eigenmeasure">[eq:transfer-eigenmeasure]</a> before expanding $L$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \rho\bar\mu
 &=\frac12\int_0^2\mu L A_{x+i}\,dx\\
 &=\frac14\int_0^2
      \mu\bigl(A_{-1/2+x/2+i/2}+A_{1/2+x/2+i/2}\bigr)\,dx\\
 &=\frac12\int_{-1/2}^{3/2}\mu A_{s+i/2}\,ds.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the substitutions $s=\pm1/2+x/2$ give two adjacent intervals. The last integrand has period two, since $$\mu A_{s+2+i/2}=\mu A_{2+i}A_{s+i/2}
                =\mu A_{s+i/2}.$$ Moving its integration interval to $[0,2]$ yields <a id="eq:half-scale-average"></a>$$\rho\bar\mu=\bar\mu A_{i/2}.
 \label{eq:half-scale-average}$$ In particular $$\bar\mu A_{2iv}=\rho^{-1}\bar\mu A_{iv},
 \qquad v>0.$$ Since $2^\beta=\rho$, the functional $$H(v)=v^\beta\bar\mu A_{iv}$$ satisfies $H(2v)=H(v)$. Moreover, each $\bar\mu A_{iv}$ remains horizontally invariant: the affine product gives $$A_{iv}A_{t+i}=A_{vt+i}A_{iv},$$ and <a href="#eq:horizontal-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:horizontal-average">[eq:horizontal-average]</a> applies to the first operator on the right.

We have obtained all horizontal translations and a dyadic scaling relation. To obtain every positive scale, set $$\nu=\int_1^2 H(v)\,\frac{dv}{v}.$$ As before, this is a finite positive functional of strictly positive mass. For every $y>0$, substitution $u=vy$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \nu A_{iy}
 &=y^{-\beta}\int_y^{2y}H(u)\,\frac{du}{u}
  =y^{-\beta}\nu.
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality follows because $t\mapsto H(e^t)$ has period $\log2$; the integral over one full period is independent of its starting point. Horizontal invariance also persists under this average. Normalize to the probability $P=\nu/\nu(\mathbf 1)$. Since $A_{x+iy}=A_{x+i}A_{iy}$, we conclude that $$PA_{x+iy}=y^{-\beta}P$$ on continuous tests.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Borel tests.

For each fixed $z=x+iy$, the expression $$E\longmapsto\int_{\mathcal K}|Q_F(z)|^2
                \mathbf 1_E(T_zF)\,dP(F)$$ defines a finite Borel measure on $\mathcal K$. Indeed, its weight and transformation are continuous in $F$, and the weight is bounded on $\mathcal K$. The identity on continuous functions identifies this measure with $y^{-\beta}P$. Equality on nonnegative simple functions, followed by monotone convergence, proves <a href="#eq:weighted-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-covariance">[eq:weighted-covariance]</a> in its stated generality. ◻

</details>

The covariance identity also applies to integrable signed tests, by their positive and negative parts. When a nonnegative test depends in a jointly Borel way on an additional parameter, we may apply the identity for each fixed parameter and then integrate by Tonelli’s theorem. These uses require only the equality of weighted measures just proved.

<a id="stmt-lem-law-moments"></a>
<div id="lem:law-moments" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable"></a>
<a id="eq:law-moments"></a>
<a id="eq:q-affine-covariance"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Moments of the logarithmic derivative). *Assume $\rho>2$, and let $P$ and $\beta$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:weighted-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weighted-law">7</a>. For $z=x+iy\in\mathbb H$, define $$q_F(z)=iy\frac{Q'_F(z)}{Q_F(z)}
       =-iy\frac{F''(z)}{F'(z)}
       =a_F(z)+ib_F(z),
 \label{eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable}$$ where $a_F,b_F$ are real. The variables $q_F(i)$ are bounded on $\mathcal K$, and $$\mathbb Ea_F(i)=-\frac\beta2,
 \qquad \mathbb Eb_F(i)=0,
 \qquad \mathbb E|q_F(i)|^2=\frac{\beta(\beta+1)}4.
 \label{eq:law-moments}$$ They also satisfy $$q_{T_zF}(w)=q_F(z\cdot w),\qquad z,w\in\mathbb H.
 \label{eq:q-affine-covariance}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-law-moments"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-law-moments</summary>

<a id="eq:inverse-derivative-mean"></a>
*Proof.* Taking $\Phi=\mathbf 1$ in <a href="#eq:weighted-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-covariance">[eq:weighted-covariance]</a> gives $$\mathbb E|Q_F(x+iy)|^2=y^{-\beta}.
 \label{eq:inverse-derivative-mean}$$ On a compact neighborhood of any interior point, the functions $Q_F,Q'_F,Q''_F$ are uniformly bounded over $\mathcal K$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-class" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-class">4</a>. These bounds control the first two real derivatives of $|Q_F|^2$, and hence justify differentiation under expectation in <a href="#eq:inverse-derivative-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inverse-derivative-mean">[eq:inverse-derivative-mean]</a>.

Holomorphicity and <a href="#eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable">[eq:logarithmic-derivative-variable]</a> give the pointwise identities $$\partial_y|Q_F|^2=\frac{2a_F}{y}|Q_F|^2,
 \qquad
 \partial_x|Q_F|^2=\frac{2b_F}{y}|Q_F|^2,
 \qquad
 \Delta|Q_F|^2=4|Q'_F|^2.$$ At $i$ we have $Q_F(i)=1$, so the $y$ derivative, the $x$ derivative, and the Euclidean Laplacian of <a href="#eq:inverse-derivative-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:inverse-derivative-mean">[eq:inverse-derivative-mean]</a> give, respectively, $$2\mathbb Ea_F(i)=-\beta,
 \qquad 2\mathbb Eb_F(i)=0,
